.

4
Discussion

In this section, we present sketches of proofs, we compare our results with prior work, we further elaborate on Algorithm
1
, Algorithm
2
and the error of higher order moment estimates. First, we discuss the convergence of the estimate
\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}
(Section
4.1
). In section
4.2
, we explain the connection between the hidden and noiseless settings on the tree structure learning problem. Later, in Section
4.3
, we present the analysis and a sketch of proof for Theorem
7
. Finally, in Section
4.4
, we provide further details about Theorem
10
, discussion about the Matching Pairs algorithm (Algorithm
2
) and the accuracy of the proposed higher order moments estimator (
47
).

4.1
Estimating the Tree Structure
\mathrm{T}

In this work, the structure learning algorithm is based on the classical Chow-Liu algorithm, and is summarized in Algorithm
1
. We can express its output as

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\mathrm{T}\in\mathcal{T}}\sum_{(i,j)\in\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}}\left|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{i,j}\right|,

(52)

(see also Section
2.6
.). The difference between Algorithm
1
and the Chow-Liu algorithm of the noiseless scheme is the use of noisy observations as input, since we consider a hidden model, whereas
Bresler and Karzand 2020
assume that observations directly from the tree-structured model are available. Further, (
52
) shows the consistency of the estimate
\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}
for sufficiently large
n
. The tree structure estimator
\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}
converges to
\mathrm{T}
when
n\to\infty
, since

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{i,j}\overset{\text{a.s.}}{=}c^{2}\mu_{i,j}.

(53)

From (
52
) and (
53
) we have (under an appropriate metric)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}\overset{\text{a.s.}}{=}\mathrm{T}.

(54)

Asymptotically, both
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{CL}}
and
\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}
converge to
\mathrm{T}
, where
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{CL}}
denotes the structure estimate from noiseless data (
q=0
). Most importantly, the Chow-Liu algorithm also returns the exact hidden structure with high probability given finite number of noisy samples. We provide the finite sample complexity bound in Theorem
5
. For a fixed probability of exact structure recovery
1-\delta
, more samples are required in the hidden model setting, compared to the noiseless one. Additionally, the difference of the sample complexity between the noisy and noiseless setting comes from Theorem
5
by comparing the bound for the values
q=0
and
q\neq 0
.

4.2
Hidden Structure Recovery and Comparison with Prior Results

Theorem
5
and Theorem
6
extend the noiseless setting
(
Bresler and Karzand 2020
, Theorem 3.2, Theorem 3.1)
to our hidden model; the noiseless results correspond to
q=0
.
In particular, in the presence of noise,
the dependence on
p
remains strictly logarithmic, that is,

\mathcal{O}(\log(p/\delta))
.
To make the connection between sufficient conditions more explicit, by setting
q=0
in (
30
) of Theorem
5
, we retrieve the corresponding structure learning result by
Bresler and Karzand 2020

exactly
: Fix a number
\delta\in(0,1)
. If the number of samples of
\mathbf{X}
satisfy the inequality

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\geq\frac{32}{\tanh^{2}\alpha\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)}\log\left(\frac{2p^{2}}{\delta}\right),

(55)

then the Chow-Liu algorithm returns
\mathrm{T}^{\mathrm{CL}}=\mathrm{T}
with probability at least
1-\delta
. An equivalent condition of (
55
) is

\displaystyle\tanh\alpha\geq\frac{4\epsilon}{\sqrt{1-\tanh\beta}}\triangleq\tau(\epsilon),\text{\ and\ }\epsilon\triangleq\sqrt{2/n\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right)},

(56)

the latter shows that the weight of weakest edge should satisfy the following inequality
\alpha>\arctanh\left(4\epsilon/\sqrt{1-\tanh\beta}\right)
(
Bresler and Karzand 2020
). For the hidden model, the equivalent extended condition for the weakest edge is

\displaystyle\tanh\alpha

\displaystyle\geq\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\tanh\beta}}{\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)}\triangleq\tau^{\dagger}(\epsilon_{\dagger}),\text{\ and\ }\epsilon_{\dagger}=\sqrt{\frac{2\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right)}{n_{\dagger}}},

(57)

(see Appendix C, Lemma
22
) Condition (
56
) is retrieved through (
57
) for
q=0
.
Note that, for
q=1/2
, the mutual information of the hidden and observable variables is zero, thus structure recovery is impossible.

Theorem
6
provides the necessary number of samples bound for exact structure recovery given noisy observations. In fact, it generalizes Theorem 3.1 by
Bresler and Karzand 2020
to the hidden setting. By fixing
q=0
, Theorem
6
recovers the noiseless case. Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
. If the number of samples of
\mathbf{X}
satisfies the inequality

\displaystyle n<\frac{1}{16}e^{2\beta}\left[\alpha\tanh(\alpha)\right]^{-1}\log\left(p\right),

(58)

then for any algorithmic mapping (estimator)
\psi
, it is true that

\displaystyle\inf_{\psi}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{T}\in\mathcal{T}\\
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{T}}(\alpha,\beta)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left(\psi\left(X_{1:n}\right)\neq\mathrm{T}\right)>\frac{1}{2}.

(59)

When there is no noise,
q=0
, we retrieve the noiseless result, while for any
q\in(0,1/2)
the sample complexity increases since
[1-(4q(1-q))^{p}]^{-1}>1
in (
32
) and for
q\to 1/2
the required number of samples
n_{\dagger}\to\infty
, which makes structure learning impossible. The ratio between the noiseless and noisy necessary conditions indicates the gap between the hidden model and the original (noiseless) setting, which reads

\displaystyle\frac{n_{\dagger}}{n}\leq[1-(4q(1-q))^{p}]^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{\eta_{\text{KL}}},

(60)

(see Appendix E.). The right hand-side of (
60
) is the strong data processing inequality for the binary symmetric channel, which was recently developed by
Polyanskiy and Wu 2017
. We continue by providing the main idea and the important steps of the proof of Theorem
7
.

4.3
Theorem
7
: A Sketch of the Proof

Recall that the indices
i,j\in\mathcal{V}
of the quantities
\mu^{\dagger}_{i,j}
and
\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{i,j}
are pair of nodes, and in fact they can be considered as one (pair) index. For sake of space we introduce the notation
\mu^{\dagger}_{e}
and
\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e}
for some
e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}
, that is consistent with our previous definition and
e
represents a pair of nodes. Theorem
7
guarantees that the estimated pairwise marginal distributions are close to the the original distributions by ensuring that the
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}
is small. In this section we provide a sketch of the proof of the Theorem and we mention the main differences between the hidden model and the noiseless case
(
Bresler and Karzand 2020
)
. The intersection of three events is sufficient to guarantee that
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}
is upper bound by
\eta>0
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{corr}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\sup_{i,j\in\mathcal{V}}\left|\mu^{\dagger}_{i,j}-\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{i,j}\right|\leq\epsilon_{\dagger}\right\},

(61)

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\left\{e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}:\left|\tanh\theta_{e}\right|\geq\frac{\tau^{\dagger}(\epsilon_{\dagger})}{\left(1-2q\right)^{2}}\right\}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}\right\},

(62)

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{cascade}}\left(\gamma_{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\left|\prod_{e\in\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(i,j)}\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e}}{(1-2q)^{2}}-\prod_{e\in\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(i,j)}\frac{\mu^{\dagger}_{e}}{(1-2q)^{2}}\right|\leq\gamma_{\dagger}:\,\,i,j\in\mathcal{V}\right\},

(63)

where (
57
) gives the definition of
\tau^{\dagger}(\epsilon_{\dagger})
. The three events are equivalent events of the noiseless case, but they are modified accordingly to guarantee accurate estimation based on noisy data. The event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{corr}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)
guarantees that the error of the correlation estimates is not greater than
\epsilon_{\dagger}
. Under the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)
all the strong edges are recovered by the Chow-Liu algorithm. Similarly to the noiseless setting, the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)
requires the Chow-Liu algorithm to recover all the strong edges, while the weak edges (those that do not satisfy the inequality in (
62
)) do not affect the accuracy of the predictive learning, even if the Chow-Liu algorithm fails to recover them. In contrast with structure learning, exact structure recovery is not necessary for the predicative learning task. In other words, even if
\alpha
is extremely small, assume
\tanh(\alpha)\leq\tau^{\dagger}(\epsilon_{\dagger})/(1-2q)^{2}
the required number of samples for accurate predictive learning will remain unaffected.

Under the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{cascade}}\left(\gamma_{\dagger}\right)
the end-to-end error along paths is no greater than
\gamma_{\dagger}
. In fact, each path between two nodes of the tree can be considered a sequence of segments with strong and weak edges. The end-to-end path error is determined by the strong edge segments of the path through the parameter
\gamma_{\dagger}
for the
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{cascade}}\left(\gamma_{\dagger}\right)
event, while the effect of weak edges parameters is controlled by the quantity
\tau^{\dagger}(\epsilon_{\dagger})
(for the segmentation of the tree and the detailed proof see
26
). Our goal is to find sufficient conditions on the parameters
\epsilon_{\dagger}
and
\gamma_{\dagger}
that guarantee that the events
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{corr}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right),\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{cascade}}\left(\gamma_{\dagger}\right)
and
\mathrm{E}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon\right)
occur with high probability.

Recall that our goal is to guarantee that the quantity
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}(\mathrm{p}(\cdot),\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(\hat{\mathrm{p}}_{\dagger}))
is smaller than a fixed number
\eta>0
with probability at least
1-\delta
. To do this, we follow the technique of prior work by
Bresler and Karzand 2020
, the triangle inequality gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(2)}\left(\mathrm{p}(\cdot),\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(\hat{\mathrm{p}}_{\dagger})\right)\leq\mathcal{L}^{(2)}\left(\mathrm{p}(\cdot),\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}\left(\mathrm{p}(\cdot)\right)\right)+\mathcal{L}^{(2)}\left(\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}\left(\mathrm{p}(\cdot)\right),\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(\hat{\mathrm{p}}_{\dagger})\right),

(64)

and we find the required number of samples such that each of the terms
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}(\mathrm{p}(\cdot),\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}\left(\mathrm{p}(\cdot)\right))
and
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}(\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}\left(\mathrm{p}(\cdot)\right),\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(\hat{\mathrm{p}}_{\dagger}))
is no greater than
\eta/2
with probability at least
1-\delta
. As we show the probability of the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{cascade}}\left(\gamma_{\dagger}\right)
(Lemma
25
, Appendix) and the
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}
(between the true and estimated distribution) can be bounded by a constant uniformly over the set of all trees and is not affected by long paths. To prove these properties of the hidden model is non-trivial and ensures that the estimation error from noisy observations does not increase exponentially along paths as someone might expect. Specifically, the first quantity at the right hand-side of inequality (
64
) represents the loss due to graph estimation error, while the second term represents the loss due to parameter estimation error. Lemma
26
(Appendix) shows that under the event

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}(\epsilon_{\dagger},\gamma_{\dagger})\triangleq\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{corr}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)\cap\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{cascade}}\left(\gamma_{\dagger}\right)\cap\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right),

(65)

if

\displaystyle\gamma_{\dagger}\leq\frac{\eta}{3}\text{ and }\epsilon_{\dagger}\leq(1-2q)^{2}e^{-\beta}\left[20\left(1+2e^{\beta}\sqrt{2\left(1-q\right)q\tanh\beta}\right)\right]^{-1}

(66)

then
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}(\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}\left(\mathrm{p}(\cdot)\right),\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(\hat{\mathrm{p}}_{\dagger}))\leq\eta/2
. Further Lemma
27
(Appendix) shows that if

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\dagger}\leq\min\left\{\frac{\eta}{16}(1-2q)^{2},\frac{(1-2q)^{2}e^{-\beta}}{24\left(1+2e^{\beta}\sqrt{2\left(1-q\right)q\tanh\beta}\right)}\right\}

(67)

then
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}\left(\mathrm{p}(\cdot),\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}\left(\mathrm{p}(\cdot)\right)\right)\leq\frac{\eta}{2}
under the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{corr}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)\cap\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)
. Both conditions (
66
) and (
67
) should be satisfied, so it is necessary to have

\displaystyle\gamma_{\dagger}\leq\frac{\eta}{3}\text{ and }\epsilon_{\dagger}\leq\min\left\{\frac{\eta}{16}(1-2q)^{2},\frac{(1-2q)^{2}e^{-\beta}}{24\left(1+2e^{\beta}\sqrt{2\left(1-q\right)q\tanh\beta}\right)}\right\}.

(68)

To guarantee that the errors
\gamma_{\dagger}
and
\epsilon_{\dagger}
are sufficient small such that (
68
) is satisfied, we need to make sure that the number of samples
n
is sufficiently large. In fact, the upper bounds on the errors translate into lower bounds on the number of samples through the concentration bounds for the events. Specifically, Lemma
18
gives a sufficient sample size to ensure that the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{corr}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)
occurs with probability at least
1-\delta
, Lemma
22
gives the concentration bound for the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)
and Lemma
25
gives the concentration bound of the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{cascade}}\left(\gamma_{\dagger}\right)
. Lemma
18
, Lemma
22
and Lemma
25
together with (
68
) give the final bound of the sample complexity (see the proof
28
)

\displaystyle n\geq

\displaystyle\max\Bigg\{\frac{512}{\eta^{2}(1-2q)^{4}},\frac{1152e^{2\beta}B(\beta,q)}{(1-2q)^{4}},\frac{48e^{4\beta}}{\eta^{2}}\Gamma(\beta,q)\Bigg\}\log\left(\frac{6p^{3}}{\delta}\right)

(69)

and its simplified but looser bound

\displaystyle n\geq

\displaystyle\max\Bigg\{\frac{512}{\eta^{2}(1-2q)^{4}},\frac{1152\left(1+3\sqrt{q}\right)^{2}e^{2\beta(1+\mathds{1}_{q\neq 0})}}{(1-2q)^{4}},\frac{48e^{4\beta}}{\eta^{2}}\mathds{1}_{q\neq 0}\Bigg\}\log\left(\frac{6p^{3}}{\delta}\right),

(70)

that provides the condition of Theorem
3
. Although the general structure of our argument follows that of the noiseless case, the presence of noise introduces several technical challenges whose solution may be of independent interest. In the sequel, we highlight the most important aspects of our approach that do not appear in the noiseless case.

The proof of Theorem 3.3 is significantly different and includes additional steps and techniques compared with the approach by
Bresler and Karzand 2020
. Specifically, Lemma
23
is new and it is necessary for the hidden model and we use it later to prove (Lemma
25
, Appendix). Lemma
24
is an non-trivial extension of the accurate estimation of edges’ correlation. Although the resulting expression seems complicated is important for the proof of Lemma
25
. In fact Lemma
25
, the proof of the concentration bound for the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{cascade}}\left(\gamma_{\dagger}\right)
, is significantly more complicated and longer than the noiseless model (see Appendix E by
Bresler and Karzand 2020
for comparison). To show this result we have to consider a martingale difference sequence and evaluate upper bounds for the conditional variance and bias of that sequence. The bias is crucial for the final result because it introduces an extra term in the final bound that does not exist in the noiseless case. It is interesting that this term does not involve any parameter related to the noise and shows how the result is affected by the structural inconsistency between the hidden and the observable layer. As a consequence, the expression of the bound (
170
) in Lemma
25
involves two inequalities to guarantee the high-probability bound. The first inequality which introduces restrictions on the parameter
\Delta
(see inequality
170
) is an attribute of the noisy case. We continue by briefly explaining one of the main technical aspects of the proof.

To begin with, consider a path of length
d\geq 2
in the original tree
\mathrm{T}
,
X_{1}-X_{2}-\cdot\cdot\cdot-X_{d+1}
and we denote the edge
(k,k+1)
as
e_{k}
, for some
k\in[d]
. Recall that
Y^{(i)}_{k}
denotes the
i^{\text{th}}
sample of
Y_{k}
and
k\in[d+1]
. We would like derive a concentration bound of the probability of the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{cascade}}\left(\gamma_{\dagger}\right)
(Lemma
25
, Appendix). To do this, first we have to consider for all
\ell\in[n]
and
k\in\{2,\ldots,d\}
the random variables

\displaystyle Z^{(\ell)}_{k}\triangleq\left(\frac{\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}}{(1-2q)^{2}}-\frac{\mu^{\dagger}_{e_{k}}}{(1-2q)^{2}}\right)\prod^{k-1}_{j=1}\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{j}}}{(1-2q)^{2}}\prod^{d}_{j=k+1}\frac{\mu^{\dagger}_{e_{j}}}{(1-2q)^{2}}.

(71)

Define the martingale difference sequence (MDS)
\{\xi_{k}^{(i)}\}
by setting
\xi_{k}^{(0)}\triangleq 0
,
\xi_{k}^{(1)}\triangleq Z^{(1)}_{k}-\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{(1)}_{k}|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}},\ldots,\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{1}}\right]
,
\xi^{(i)}_{k}\triangleq Z^{(i)}_{k}-\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{(i)}_{k}|Z^{(i-1)}_{k},\ldots,Z^{(1)}_{k},\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}},\ldots,\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{1}}\right]
. Let
\mathcal{F}^{k}_{i-1}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
Z^{(i-1)}_{k},\ldots,Z^{(1)}_{k},\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}},\ldots,\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{1}}
. Then the pair
(\xi^{(i)}_{k},\mathcal{F}^{k}_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,n}
is an MDS. In contrast with the noiseless case,
the conditional means are not zero
, which makes the problem significantly harder. To proceed, we apply a concentration bound for supermartingales (generalized Bennett’s inequality) by
Fan et al. 2012
.

Secondly we have to evaluate the following expression

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Y^{(\ell)}_{k}Y^{(\ell)}_{k+1}=\pm 1\Big|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}},\ldots,\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\frac{1\pm\mu^{\dagger}_{e_{k}}}{2}\frac{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}}\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}}}{1-(\mu^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}})^{2}}+\mu^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}}\frac{1\pm\mu_{e_{k}}}{2}\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}}-\mu^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}}}{1-(\mu^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}})^{2}}.

(72)

In the noiseless case, the product variables
X^{(\ell)}_{k}X^{(\ell)}_{k+1}
are independent, leading to a simple expression for this probability (see Lemma
13
, Appendix).
The closed form expression of (
72
) is given by Lemma
23
. Finally, the expectations
\mathbb{E}\left[Z^{(i)}_{k}|Z^{(i-1)}_{k},\ldots,Z^{(1)}_{k},\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{k-1}},\ldots,\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e_{1}}\right]
are not zero, however when
n\to\infty
, they approach zero. As a consequence, a bias exists that affects the sample complexity by introducing an additional term in the bound that that does not appear in the noiseless case, the quantity
e^{4\beta}/\eta^{2}
(see Equation
69
and Equation
70
).

Finally, we continue by bounding the norm
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}
between the true and estimated distribution in Appendix E. The proof of Lemma
26
shows that in the noisy setting as well, the
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}
can be bounded by a constant uniformly over the set of all trees and it is not affected by long paths. This property of the hidden model is highly non-trivial and ensures that the estimation error from noisy observations does not increases along paths as someone might expect. Lemma
27
follows the corresponding approach of Lemma 6.1 by
Bresler and Karzand 2020
and we provide only the required for the noisy setting differences. In Theorem
28
, we combine the Lemmata of Appendices
D
and
E
, we find the appropriate choice of the parameter
\Delta
that satisfies the necessary conditions of Lemma
25
and we derive the final sample complexity bound. For further details about the proof of the main result see Appendix, Section
D
and Section
E
.

4.4
Estimating Higher Order Moments

Our results also provide an analogue of Isserlis’ Theorem (Theorem
10
) and the
Matching Pairs
algorithm, which returns the set
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
in (
45
). We provide a short proof sketch for the bound on the error of estimation (
48
).

Proof sketch of Theorem
10
:
We prove that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
always exists (when
k
is even) by induction (see Appendix A, Lemma
15
). We define the set of edges
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
as the union of the edge-disjoint paths
4
4

4

By edge-disjoint paths we refer to paths with no common edges.

\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})=\cup_{w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})}\mathrm{path}(w,w^{\prime})
. Combining the set
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
together with the independent products property (see Lemma
13
), we derive the final expression (see Appendix A, proof of Theorem
10
). Given the tree structure
\mathrm{T}
and the correlations
\mu_{e}
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
, we can calculate the higher order expectations. Notice that the collection of edge-disjoint paths
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}
depends on the tree structure and as a consequence an algorithm is required to discover those paths. Different matching algorithms can be considered to find the set
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}
. We propose Algorithm
2
which is simple and has low complexity of
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{E}|)
.

Matching Pairs Algorithm:
Algorithm
2
requires as input the tree and the set of nodes
\mathcal{V^{\prime}}\equiv\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\subset\mathcal{V}
, and returns the set of edges
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(V^{\prime})
. For each node in the tree, a flag variable is assigned to each node and indicates if the corresponding node is a candidate for the final set
C_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
at the current step of the algorithm. The candidate nodes have to be matched with other nodes of the tree, such that the pairs generate edge-disjoint paths. Initially, the candidate nodes are the nodes of the set
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. Starting from the nodes which appear in the deepest level of the tree, we “move” them to their ancestor. At each step, if two candidate nodes appear at the same point, we match them as pair, we store the pair in the set
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
and we remove both of them from the set
\mathcal{V^{\prime}}
. We continue until
\mathcal{V^{\prime}}\equiv\emptyset
. The complexity of Algorithm
2
is
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{E}|)
. Finally, Theorem
10
can be extended to any forest
F
structure by considering the set
\mathcal{CP}_{\text{F}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
instead of
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
, where
\mathcal{CP}_{\text{F}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})\triangleq\cup_{i}\mathcal{CP}_{\text{T}_{i}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
and
T_{i}
is the
i^{\text{th}}
connected tree of the forest.

Estimation error of higher order moments:
Inequality (
48
) bounds the error of estimation by the small set Total Variation (ssTV), that is guaranteed to be less than
\eta>0
by Theorem
7
. Additionally, the bound on the error of the estimation in (
48
) can be found as follows

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{s\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}X_{s}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{s\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}X_{s}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\mkern-200.0mu=\left|\prod_{e\in\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k})}\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e}}{(1-2q)^{2}}-\prod_{e\in\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k})}\mu_{e}\right|

(73)

\displaystyle\mkern-200.0mu=\left|\prod_{e\in\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k})}\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e}}{(1-2q)^{2}}-\prod_{e\in\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k})}\frac{\mu^{\dagger}_{e}}{(1-2q)^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\mkern-200.0mu=\left|\prod_{e\in\underset{\left\{w,w^{\prime}\right\}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})}{\bigcup}\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(w,w^{\prime})}\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e}}{(1-2q)^{2}}-\prod_{e\in\underset{\left\{w,w^{\prime}\right\}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})}{\bigcup}\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(w,w^{\prime})}\frac{\mu^{\dagger}_{e}}{(1-2q)^{2}}\right|

(74)

\displaystyle\mkern-200.0mu=\left|\prod_{\left\{w,w^{\prime}\right\}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(w,w^{\prime})}\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e}}{(1-2q)^{2}}-\prod_{\left\{w,w^{\prime}\right\}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(w,w^{\prime})}\frac{\mu^{\dagger}_{e}}{(1-2q)^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\mkern-200.0mu\leq 2|\mathcal{V^{\prime}}|\mathcal{L}^{(2)}\left(\mathrm{p}(\cdot),\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(\hat{\mathrm{p}}_{\dagger})\right),

(75)

where (
73
) holds due to (
45
) and (
47
), (
74
) comes from (
44
) and the last inequality (
75
) is being proved by
Bresler and Karzand 2020
. Thus, if we can accurately estimate the distribution under the sense
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}\left(P,\Pi{}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}(\hat{\mathrm{p}}_{\dagger})\right)\leq\eta^{\prime}
, for a sufficiently small positive number
\eta^{\prime}
, then by using (
47
) and choosing
\eta^{\prime}\leq\eta/(2|\mathcal{V}^{\prime}|)
, Theorem
7
guarantees accurate estimates for higher order moments with probability at least
1-\delta
.

5
Conclusion

We have considered and analyzed the problem of predictive learning on hidden tree-structures from noisy observations, using the well-known Chow-Liu algorithm. In particular, we derived sample complexity guarantees for exact structure learning and marginal distributions estimation. Our bounds extend prior work (see
Bresler and Karzand 2020
) to the hidden model, by introducing the cross-over probability
q
of the
\mathrm{BSC}(q)^{p}
. Our results exactly reduce to the noiseless setting when
q=0
, and the explicit expressions of the bounds are also continuous functions of
q
. Additionally, by applying a graph property for tree structures and a probabilistic property for Ising models, we derived an equivalent of the well-known Isserlis’ theorem for Gaussian distributions, which yields to a consistent high-order moments estimator for Ising models. Further, we considered simulations based on synthetic data to validate our theoretical results. Our theoretical bounds exactly match with the experiment. indicating that our results correctly characterize the dependence on the model parameters.

Our results show that the estimated structure statistic
\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}
is essential for successful statistical inference on the hidden (or observable) layer, while the sample complexity with respect to number of nodes and probability of error remains strictly logarithmic, as in the noiseless case. Our hidden setting constitutes a first step towards more technically challenging and potentially more realistic statistical models, such as, for instance, structure and distribution learning when the noise is generated by an erasure channel, or when the underlying hidden tree structured distribution has a larger, or even uncountable, support.

Preliminaries and Outline of Proof

The chart in Figure

shows the various dependencies of the Lemmata and intermediate results either considered or developed in this paper, and the resulting Theorems. The proofs can be found in the corresponding section of the Appendix.

Stream mapping of the results

For completeness, we start with some properties that hold for any distribution with support
\{-1,+1\}^{p}
and tree-structured graphical model
(
Lauritzen 1996
)
. Later we derive explicit formulas for the Ising model (
10
).

Lemma 12
.

Any distribution
\mathrm{p}(\mathbf{x})
with respect to a forest
F=\left(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}\right)
, where
\mathbf{x}\in\{-1,1\}^{p}
and uniform marginals
\mathbb{P}\left(X_{i}=\pm 1\right)=1/2
, for all
i\in\mathcal{V}

can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1+x_{i}x_{j}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]}{2}.

(76)

Proof.

We prove the result for an arbitrary tree
\mathrm{T}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
and then we extend it to any forest structure by applying cuts to
\mathcal{E}
. The distribution factorizes according to the tree structure
\mathrm{T}

and under the assumption of no external field (uniform marginal distributions), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i\in V}\mathrm{p}\left(x_{i}\right)\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{\mathrm{p}(x_{i},x_{j})}{\mathrm{p}(x_{i})\mathrm{p}(x_{j})}

\displaystyle=2^{p-2}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1+x_{i}x_{j}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]}{4}

(77)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1+x_{i}x_{j}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]}{2}.

(78)

(
77
) holds since the joint distribution of any pair
(X_{i},X_{j})
of distinct nodes
i,j\in\mathcal{V}
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}(x_{i},x_{j})=\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{1}_{X_{i}=x_{i}}\boldsymbol{1}_{X_{j}=x_{j}}\right]=\frac{1+x_{i}x_{j}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}]}{4}.

(79)

By setting
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}]=0
for some
(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}
we derive the distribution with respect to a forest generated by cutting the edge
(i,j)
of
\mathrm{T}
.
∎

In Lemma
13
we prove two fundamental properties of the model, the independence of the random variables
\{X_{i}X_{j}:(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}\}
and the
correlation decay property
(CDP). To the best of our knowledge, these properties are known but there is no reference for the corresponding proofs in the literature.

Lemma 13
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a random binary vector in
\{-1,+1\}^{p}
drawn according to a forest-structured distribution
\mathrm{p}(\cdot)
with uniform marginal distributions on each entry
X_{i}
for
i\in[p]
. Then the elements of the collection of
|\mathcal{E}|
random variables
\{X_{i}X_{j}:(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}\}
, are independent. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]=\prod_{e\in\mathrm{path}\left(i,j\right)}\mu_{e},

(80)

so the Correlation Decay Property (CDP) holds since
|\mu_{e}|\leq 1
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}.

Proof.

Let
(i_{r})^{p}_{r=1}
be an arbitrary permutation of
\boldsymbol{\ell}=\{1,2,\ldots,p\}
. Notice that the singletons
\{i_{r}\}
,
r=1,\dots,p
form a partition of
\boldsymbol{\ell}
. Then, the set of edges
\mathcal{E}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\left(i_{r},j_{r}\right)^{p}_{r=2},\quad\text{ and }j_{1}=\emptyset,\quad j_{r}\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r-1}\}\subset\boldsymbol{\ell}.

(81)

(
81
) defines a tree
\mathrm{T}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
with root the node
i_{1}
(since
j_{1}=\emptyset
). For the first part, it is sufficient to show that for any
\{c_{r}:r=2,3,\ldots,p\}\in\{-1,+1\}^{p-1}
, the following holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap^{p}_{r=2}\{x_{i_{r}}x_{j_{r}}=c_{r}\}\right)=\prod^{p}_{r=2}\mathbb{P}\left(x_{i_{r}}x_{j_{r}}=c_{r}\right).

(82)

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap^{p}_{r=2}\{X_{i_{r}}X_{j_{r}}=c_{r}\}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x}:x_{i_{r}}x_{j_{r}}=c_{r}|^{p}_{r=2}}p\left(\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x}:x_{i_{r}}x_{j_{r}}=c_{r}|^{p}_{r=2}}\frac{1}{2}\prod^{p}_{r=2}\frac{1+x_{i_{r}}x_{j_{r}}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i_{r}}X_{j_{r}}\right]}{2}

(83)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x}:x_{i_{r}}=c_{r}x_{j_{r}}|^{p}_{r=2}}\frac{1}{2}\prod^{p}_{r=2}\frac{1+x_{i_{r}}x_{j_{r}}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i_{r}}X_{j_{r}}\right]}{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{i_{1}}\in\{-1,+1\}}\frac{1}{2}\prod^{p}_{r=2}\frac{1+c_{r}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i_{r}}X_{j_{r}}\right]}{2}=\prod^{p}_{r=2}\mathbb{P}\left(X_{i_{r}}X_{j_{r}}=c_{r}\right),

(84)

(
83
) comes from (
81
) and Lemma
12
and the last from (
79
). For the second part of the statement note that for all
i,j\in\mathcal{V}
there exists a unique path
\{i,k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{\ell},j\}
from
i
to
j
. Define the variable
\boldsymbol{1}_{(i,j)}\triangleq(X_{k_{1}}X_{k_{1}})(X_{k_{2}}X_{k_{2}})\ldots(X_{k_{\ell}}X_{k_{\ell}})
, which is equal to
1
almost surely, since
\mathbf{X}\in\{-1,+1\}^{p}
.
5
5

5

\boldsymbol{1}_{(\cdot)}
should not be confused with
\mathds{1}_{\boldsymbol{A}}
, where the last denotes the indicator function of a set
A
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}\boldsymbol{1}_{(i,j)}X_{j}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}(X_{k_{1}}X_{k_{1}})(X_{k_{2}}X_{k_{2}})\ldots(X_{k_{\ell}}X_{k_{\ell}})X_{j}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{k_{1}}]\left(\prod^{\ell-1}_{m=1}\mathbb{E}[X_{k_{m}}X_{k_{m+1}}]\right)\mathbb{E}[X_{\ell}X_{j}]=\prod_{e\in\mathrm{path}\left(i,j\right)}\mu_{e},

(85)

and (
85
) comes from (
82
) and completes the proof.
∎

The next lemma relates the pairwise correlations to the parameters of the Ising model.

Lemma 14
.

An equivalent expression of (
10
) is the following

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\left[1+x_{i}x_{j}\tanh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\right]}{\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\left[1+x_{i}x_{j}\tanh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\right]}\hskip 8.5359pt\mathbf{x}\in\{-1,1\}^{p}.

(86)

Further, for a tree-structure Ising model
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i},X_{j}\right]=\tanh\left(\theta_{ij}\right),
for all
\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

We can write
\exp\left(\theta_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right)
as

\displaystyle\exp\left(\theta_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(\theta_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right)+\exp\left(-\theta_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right)}{2}+\frac{\exp\left(\theta_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right)-\exp\left(-\theta_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right)}{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\left(\theta_{ij}\right)+\exp\left(-\theta_{ij}\right)}{2}+x_{i}x_{j}\frac{\exp\left(\theta_{ij}\right)-\exp\left(-\theta_{ij}\right)}{2}

(87)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\left[1+x_{i}x_{j}\tanh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\right],

(88)

(
87
) holds because
x_{i}x_{j}\in\{-1,+1\}
.
The partition function can be written as

\displaystyle Z\left(\theta\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\exp\left(\theta_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\cosh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\left[1+x_{i}x_{j}\tanh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\cosh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\left[1+x_{i}x_{j}\tanh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\right]=2^{p}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\cosh\left(\theta_{ij}\right).

(89)

Notice that
\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\left[1+x_{i}x_{j}\tanh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\right]=2^{p}
under the tree-structure assumption. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x})=\frac{\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\exp\left(\theta_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}\right)}{Z\left(\theta\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\cosh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\left[1+x_{i}x_{j}\tanh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\right]}{2^{p}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\cosh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)}

(90)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1+x_{i}x_{j}\tanh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)}{2},

(91)

(
88
) and (
89
) give (
90
) and
|\mathcal{E}|=p-1
gives (
91
).
Finally

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]\overset{\eqref{eq:Ising_model_zero_external}}{=}\frac{\partial\ln Z\left(\theta\right)}{\partial\theta_{ij}}\overset{\eqref{eq:partition_tanh}}{=}\frac{\partial\ln\left[2^{p}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\cosh\left(\theta_{ij}\right)\right]}{\partial\theta_{ij}}=\tanh(\theta_{ij}),\quad\forall(i,j)\in\mathcal{E},

(92)

and the latter gives the second part of the Lemma.
∎

Lemma 15
.

Let
\mathcal{V^{\prime}}
be a set of nodes such that
\mathcal{V^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{V}
and
|\mathcal{V^{\prime}}|\in 2\mathbb{N}
. Then it exists a set
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
of
|\mathcal{V^{\prime}}|/2
pairs of nodes of
\mathcal{V^{\prime}}
, such that any two distinct pairs
(w,w^{\prime})
,
(v,v^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
are pairwise disjoint (their paths have no commons edge), that is,

\displaystyle\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(w,w^{\prime})\cap\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(v,v^{\prime})=\emptyset,\quad\forall(w,w^{\prime}),(v,v^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}}):\text{ }(w,w^{\prime})\not\equiv(v,v^{\prime}).

(93)

Proof.

We prove the existence of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
by contradiction. Assume that the two distinct paths
\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(w,u^{\prime})
,
\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(u,w^{\prime})
share at least one edge. Let their common sub-path be
\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(z,z^{\prime})
, Figure
8
and note that
z
and
z^{\prime}
do not necessarily differ from
w,w^{\prime},u,u^{\prime}
. Notice that the common sub-path is unique (acyclic graph). Then we can always consider the permutation of the endpoints which gives the edge-disjoint paths
\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(w,u)
and
\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(w^{\prime},u^{\prime})
. Now the paths
\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(w,u)
and
\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}(w^{\prime},u^{\prime})
are disjoint, however it is possible that one of them or both, contain sub-paths with common edges. Then, we similarly proceed by removing the common sub-paths as previously. The set of common edges strictly decreases through the process, which terminates when there are only paths with no common edge.
∎

Figure 8:
Proof of the existence of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{V^{\prime}})
, Lemma
15

Theorem 16

(Theorem
10
)
.

Assume
\mathbf{X}\sim\mathrm{p}(\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{T}}(\alpha,\beta)
,
\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}\}\subset\mathcal{V}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\ldots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\begin{cases}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k})}\mu_{e},&\forall k\in 2\mathbb{N}\\
0,&\forall k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1.\end{cases}

(94)

Recall that the set of edges
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
is a collection of
k/2
edge-disjoint paths with endpoints pairs of the nodes
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
for each path. Given a tree structure
\mathrm{T}
,
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
is found by running Algorithm
2
on
\mathrm{T}
.

Proof.

Even
k
.
We proceed by showing that the Algorithm
2
returns the unique set
\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}
. When
k=2
the expression is proved in Lemma
13
. For
k>2
we proceed by using Lemmas
13
and
15
. For all
i,j\in\mathcal{V}
there exists a unique path
\{i,k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{\ell},j\}
from
i
to
j
. Define as previously the variable
\boldsymbol{1}_{(i,j)}\triangleq(X_{k_{1}}X_{k_{1}})(X_{k_{2}}X_{k_{2}})\ldots(X_{k_{\ell}}X_{k_{\ell}})
, which is equal to
1
almost surely, and define the set of nodes
\mathcal{V^{\prime}}\triangleq\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}\}
. Without loss of generality we assume that the variables in the product
X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\ldots X_{i_{k}}
are ordered such such that the pairs
X_{i_{j}},X_{i_{j+1}}
for all
j\in\{1,3,5,\ldots,k-1\}\triangleq[k-1]^{\text{odd}}
form edge-disjoint paths (Lemma
15
), in other words

\displaystyle\mathrm{path}(i_{j},i_{j+1})\cap\mathrm{path}(i_{j^{\prime}},i_{j^{\prime}+1})=\emptyset,\forall j\neq j^{\prime}\in[k-1]^{\text{odd}}.

(95)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\ldots X_{i_{k}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i_{1}}\mathbbm{1}_{(i_{1},i_{2})}X_{i_{2}}X_{i_{3}}\mathbbm{1}_{(i_{3},i_{4})}X_{i_{4}}\cdots X_{i_{k-1}}\mathbbm{1}_{(i_{k-1},i_{k})}X_{i_{k}}\right]

(96)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\in[k-1]^{\text{odd}}}\mathbb{E}[X_{i_{j}}\mathbbm{1}_{(i_{j},i_{j+1})}X_{i_{j+1}}]

(97)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\in[k-1]^{\text{odd}}}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{path}\left(i_{j},i_{j+1}\right)}\mu_{e}

(98)

\displaystyle=\prod_{e\in\mathcal{CP}_{\mathrm{T}}(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k})}\mu_{e},

(99)

where (
96
) and (
97
) come from (
80
), and (
98
) holds because of (
95
).

Odd
k
.
Lemma
12
gives
\mathrm{p}(\mathbf{x})=2^{-p}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}(1+x_{i}x_{j}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]).
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\ldots X_{i_{k}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\{-1,+1\}^{i_{k}}}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\ldots x_{i_{k}}\prod_{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{1+x_{i}x_{j}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]}{2}=0,

(100)

gives the second part of (
94
).
∎

Lemma 17
.

The mutual information of
X_{i},X_{j}\in\{-1,+1\}
is symmetric function of the correlation
\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]
and increasing with respect to
\left|\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]\right|
,

\displaystyle I\left(X_{i},X_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}\left(\left(1-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]\right)^{1-\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]}\left(1+\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]\right)^{1+\mathbb{E}\left[X_{i}X_{j}\right]}\right).

(101)

The proof can be derived through the definition of
I\left(X_{i},X_{j}\right)
and the expression (
79
), under the assumption of uniform marginal distributions.

Appendix B
Bounding the Probability of Mis-Estimating Correlations

The following lemma bounds the probability that the estimated pairwise correlations in the graph deviate from their true values. This follows from standard concentration of measure arguments.

Lemma 18
.

Fix
\delta>0
. Then for any
\epsilon_{\dagger}>0
, if

\displaystyle n_{\dagger}\geq 2\log\left(p^{2}/\delta\right)/\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},

(102)

then the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{corr}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)
defined in (
61
) holds with high probability:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{corr}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)\right)\geq 1-\delta=1-p^{2}\exp\left(\frac{-n_{\dagger}\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}}{2}\right).

(103)

Proof.

Let
Z_{\dagger}^{(i)}
be
the
i_{\text{th}}
sample of
Z_{\dagger}=Y_{w}Y_{\bar{w}}=N_{w}X_{w}N_{\bar{w}}X_{\bar{w}}
.
Then
\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{i,j}=\frac{1}{n_{\dagger}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}Z_{\dagger}^{(i)}=\frac{1}{n_{\dagger}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}N_{w}^{(i)}X_{w}^{(i)}N_{\bar{w}}^{(i)}X_{\bar{w}}^{(i)}
for all
i\neq j\in\mathcal{V}
. Then Hoeffding’s inequality and union bound over all pairs of nodes
{p\choose 2}<p^{2}/2
give (
103
).
∎

For the rest of the paper we consider
\epsilon_{\dagger}=\sqrt{2\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right)/n_{\dagger}}
, which satisfies Lemma
18
. We apply Lemmata
19
,
20
,
21
to Lemma
22
to bound the required number of samples for exact structure recovery using noisy observations of the hidden model. To analyze the error event we use the “Two trees lemma” of
Bresler and Karzand 2020
. Informally, if two maximum spanning trees
\mathrm{T}
,
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
differ in how a pair of nodes are connected then there exists at least one edge in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}
which does not exist in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}^{\prime}}
and vice versa. Lemma
19
characterizes errors in the Chow-Liu in terms of correlations.

Lemma 19
.

Suppose the error event
\{\mathrm{T}\neq\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}\}
holds and let
f\triangleq\left(w,\bar{w}\right)
be an edge such that
f\in\mathrm{T}
and
f\notin\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}
. Then there exists an edge
g\triangleq(u,\bar{u})\in\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}
and
g\notin\mathrm{T}
such that
f\in\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}\left(u,\bar{u}\right)

and
g\in\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}}\left(w,\bar{w}\right)
and

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}Z_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}M_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right)

\displaystyle<0,

(104)

where
Z_{f,u,\bar{u}}\triangleq Y_{w}Y_{\bar{w}}-Y_{u}Y_{\bar{u}}
and
M_{f,u,\bar{u}}\triangleq Y_{w}Y_{\bar{w}}+Y_{u}Y_{\bar{u}}
.

Proof.

Using similar approaches to the procedures as in
(
Bresler and Karzand 2020
, Lemmata 8.2, 8.3)
we have that the condition
\left|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{f}\right|\leq\left|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{g}\right|
implies

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq\left|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{f}\right|^{2}-\left|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{g}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{f}-\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{g}\right)\left(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{f}+\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{\dagger}^{2}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}N_{w}^{(i)}X_{w}^{(i)}N_{\bar{w}}^{(i)}X_{\bar{w}}^{(i)}-N_{u}^{(i)}X_{u}^{(i)}N_{\bar{u}}^{(i)}X_{\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}N_{w}^{(i)}X_{w}^{(i)}N_{\bar{w}}^{(i)}X_{\bar{w}}^{(i)}+N_{w}^{(i)}X_{u}^{(i)}N_{\bar{u}}^{(i)}X_{\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{\dagger}^{2}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}Z_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}M_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right).

(105)

∎

Setting

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\dagger}

\displaystyle\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{2\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right)}{n_{\dagger}}}

(106)

\displaystyle\tau^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-(1-2q)^{4}\tanh\beta}}{1-\tanh\beta}

(107)

\displaystyle\mu_{e}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}\right],

(108)

we have that
\mu_{A}
is defined through the following relationship

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}-X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]=\mu_{e}(1-\mu_{A}),

(109)

and

\displaystyle\mu^{\dagger}_{A}

\displaystyle\triangleq(1-2q)^{4}\mu_{A}.

(110)

In Lemmata
20
,
21
we derive two concentration of measure inequalities for the variables
Z_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}
,
M_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}
. In fact, we have that the event

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{Z}\triangleq\left\{\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}-n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right]\right|\leq n_{\dagger}\max\left\{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right\}:\forall e\in\mathcal{E}\text{ and }\forall u,\bar{u}\in\mathcal{V}\right\}

(111)

happens with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}
and the event

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{M}\triangleq\left\{\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}M_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}-n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right]\right|\leq n_{\dagger}\max\left\{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right\}:\forall e\in\mathcal{E}\text{ and }\forall u,\bar{u}\in\mathcal{V}\right\}

(112)

happens with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta^{\prime\prime}}{2}
. The parameters
\epsilon_{\dagger}
and
\mu_{A}
, defined below, are decreasing functions of
n_{\dagger}
. Finally, we apply the union bound to guarantee that the event
E_{Z}\cup E_{M}
happens with probability at least
1-\delta
, where
\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}+\frac{\delta^{\prime\prime}}{2}\leq 2\max\{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2},\frac{\delta^{\prime\prime}}{2}\}\triangleq\delta
. The union bound is first applied over all tuples
(w,\bar{w},u,\bar{u})
in Lemmata
20
and
21
and then for the events
E_{Z}
and
E_{M}
.

Lemma 20
.

Fix
\delta>0
and let
\epsilon_{\dagger}
be given by (
106
). For all pairs of vertices
u,\bar{u}\in V
and edges
e=(w,\bar{w})
in the path
\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}\left(u,\bar{u}\right)
from
u
to
\bar{u}
,
given
n_{\dagger}
samples
Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(1)},Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(2)},...,Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(n)}
of

Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}=Y_{w}Y_{\bar{w}}-Y_{u}Y_{\bar{u}}
, it is true that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}-n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right]\right|\leq n_{\dagger}\max\left\{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right\}\right)\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{2},

(113)

where
A=\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}\left(u,\bar{u}\right)\backslash\left\{e\right\}
.

Proof.

The proof is an application of Bernstein’s inequality. First, it is true that

\displaystyle Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}

\displaystyle=X_{w}N_{w}X_{\bar{w}}N_{\bar{w}}-N_{u}X_{u}N_{\bar{u}}X_{\bar{u}}

\displaystyle=N_{w}X_{w}N_{\bar{w}}X_{\bar{w}}\left(1-N_{w}X_{w}N_{\bar{w}}X_{\bar{w}}N_{u}X_{u}N_{\bar{u}}X_{\bar{u}}\right).

(114)

Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}-X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\mu_{e}\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)

(115)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{w}N_{w}X_{\bar{w}}N_{\bar{w}}-N_{\bar{u}}X_{u}N_{\bar{u}}X_{\bar{u}}\right)^{2}\right]-\left[\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}-X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1+1-2X_{w}N_{w}X_{\bar{w}}N_{\bar{w}}N_{u}X_{u}N_{\bar{u}}X_{\bar{u}}\right]-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}-X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=2-2\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}N_{w}X_{\bar{w}}N_{\bar{w}}N_{u}X_{u}N_{\bar{u}}X_{\bar{u}}\right]-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}-X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=2-2\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}-X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=2-2\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mu_{A}-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\left(\mu_{e}\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=2-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\left[2\mu_{A}+\mu_{e}^{2}\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)^{2}\right].

(116)

Using the expressions for the mean and the variance, we apply Bernstein’s
inequality
(
Bennett 1962
)
for the noisy setting: for all
i\in[n_{\dagger}]
we have
\left|Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}-\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right]\right|\leq M

almost surely. Then, Bernstein’s inequality gives, for all
t>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}-n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right]\right|\leq t\right]

\displaystyle\geq 1-2\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2n_{\dagger}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)+\frac{2}{3}Mt}\right),\quad\forall t>0.

(117)

Choose a
\delta>0
and find
t
such that

\displaystyle\delta/2

\displaystyle=2\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2n_{\dagger}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)+\frac{2}{3}Mt}\right).

After some algebra, we have

\displaystyle\log\frac{4}{\delta}

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{2}}{2n_{\dagger}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)+\frac{2}{3}Mt}

From this we can solve for
t
:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=t^{2}-\frac{2}{3}Mt\log\frac{4}{\delta}-2n_{\dagger}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)\log\frac{4}{\delta}

\displaystyle t_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{2}{3}M\log\frac{4}{\delta}\pm\sqrt{\left(\frac{2}{3}M\log\frac{4}{\delta}\right)^{2}+8n_{\dagger}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)\log\frac{4}{\delta}}}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}M\log\frac{4}{\delta}\pm\sqrt{\left(\frac{1}{3}M\log\frac{4}{\delta}\right)^{2}+2n_{\dagger}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)\log\frac{4}{\delta}}.

(118)

Since
t>0
, we have, setting
M=4
:

\displaystyle t=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{3}\log\frac{4}{\delta}+\sqrt{\left(\frac{4}{3}\log\frac{4}{\delta}\right)^{2}+2n_{\dagger}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)\log\frac{4}{\delta}}.

(119)

If the probability of the union

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\forall u,\bar{u,}w,\bar{w:}\left(w,\bar{w}\right)\in\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}\left(u,\bar{u}\right)}

\displaystyle\left\{\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}-n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right]\right|\geq t\right\}

is at most
\frac{\delta}{2p^{3}}
, then the union bound gives
probability at most
\frac{\delta}{2}
. Also,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(Z_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)

\displaystyle=2-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\left[2\mu_{A}+\mu_{e}^{2}\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}2\mu_{A}-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mu_{e}^{2}\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}2\mu_{A}+0

\displaystyle=2\left(1-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mu_{A}\right)

\displaystyle=2\left(1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}\right).

(120)

From (
119
) and (
120
), we have

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3}\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}+\sqrt{\left(\frac{4}{3}\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}\right)^{2}+4n_{\dagger}\left(1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}\right)\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{3}\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}+\sqrt{4n_{\dagger}\left(1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}\right)\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}},

(121)

which implies that

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=n_{\dagger}\left(\frac{4}{3n_{\dagger}}\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}+\sqrt{\left(\frac{4}{3n_{\dagger}}\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}\right)^{2}+\frac{4}{n_{\dagger}}\left(1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}\right)\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq n_{\dagger}\left(\frac{8}{3n_{\dagger}}\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}+\sqrt{\frac{4}{n_{\dagger}}\left(1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}\right)\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}}\right).

(122)

Define
\epsilon_{\dagger}=\sqrt{\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right)2/n_{\dagger}}
(as it
is defined in
Bresler and Karzand 2020
), then we get

\displaystyle t\leq

\displaystyle n_{\dagger}\left(4\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}+2\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right)\leq n_{\dagger}\max\left\{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right\}.

(123)

∎

Lemma
21
gives the concentration of measure bound for the event
E_{M}
defined in (
112
).

Lemma 21
.

Fix
\delta>0
and let
\epsilon_{\dagger}
be given by (
106
). For all pairs of vertices
u,\bar{u}\in V
and edges
e=(w,\bar{w})
in the path
\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}\left(u,\bar{u}\right)
from
u
to
\bar{u}
,
given
n_{\dagger}
samples
M_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(1)},M_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(2)},...,M_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(n)}
of

M_{e,u,\bar{u}}=Y_{w}Y_{\bar{w}}+Y_{u}Y_{\bar{u}}
, it is true that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}M_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}-n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right]\right|\leq n_{\dagger}\max\left\{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right\}\right)\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{2},

(124)

A=\mathrm{path}_{\mathrm{T}}\left(u,\bar{u}\right)\backslash\left\{e\right\}
.

Proof.

Similarly to the prior Lemma, we calculate the mean and the variance as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[M_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}+X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]=\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\mu_{e}\left(1+\mu_{A}\right)

(125)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(M_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(M_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(M_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{w}N_{w}X_{\bar{w}}N_{\bar{w}}+N_{u}X_{u}N_{\bar{u}}X_{\bar{u}}\right)^{2}\right]-\left[\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}+X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1+1+2X_{w}N_{w}X_{\bar{w}}N_{\bar{w}}N_{u}X_{u}N_{\bar{u}}X_{\bar{u}}\right]-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}+X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=2+2\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}N_{w}X_{\bar{w}}N_{\bar{w}}N_{u}X_{u}N_{\bar{u}}X_{\bar{u}}\right]-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}+X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=2+2\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{w}X_{\bar{w}}+X_{u}X_{\bar{u}}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=2+2\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mu_{A}-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\left(\mu_{e}\left(1+\mu_{A}\right)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=2+\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\left[2\mu_{A}-\mu_{e}^{2}\left(1+\mu_{A}\right)^{2}\right].

(126)

By applying Bernstein’s inequality and we get that for any
t>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}M_{e,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}-n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right]\right|\leq t\right]

\displaystyle\geq 1-2\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2n_{\dagger}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(M_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)+\frac{2}{3}Mt}\right).

Similarly, we find

\displaystyle t\leq

\displaystyle n_{\dagger}\left(\frac{8}{3n_{\dagger}}\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{n_{\dagger}}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(M_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)\log\frac{4p^{3}}{\delta}}\right)

(127)

and

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\nolimits\left(M_{e,u,\bar{u}}\right)=

\displaystyle 2+\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\left[2\mu_{A}-\mu_{e}^{2}\left(1+\mu_{A}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2+\left(1-2q\right)^{4}2\mu_{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left(1+\mu^{\dagger}_{A}\right).

(128)

We define
\epsilon_{\dagger}\triangleq\sqrt{\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right)2/n_{\dagger}}
,
then

\displaystyle t\leq

\displaystyle n_{\dagger}\left(4\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}+2\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right)\leq n_{\dagger}\max\left\{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right\},

(129)

which completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Recovering Strong Edges

In Lemma
22
, we define the set of strong edges for the hidden model and show that the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)
defined in (
62
) occurs with high probability. That is, only the strong edges are guaranteed to exist in the estimated structure
\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}

We also find a lower bound for the necessary number of samples for exact structure recovery. In fact we have
n_{\dagger}\geq n
, as expected. Our bounds coincide with the noiseless case
(
Bresler and Karzand 2020
)
by setting the noise level
q=0
.

Lemma 22
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
, and let
\epsilon_{\dagger}=\sqrt{2\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right)/n_{\dagger}}
, for any
n_{\dagger}>0
. Consider the set of strong edges

\displaystyle\tau^{\dagger}\triangleq\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\tanh\beta}}{\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)}\text{ and }\left\{\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}:\left|\tanh\theta_{ij}\right|\geq\frac{\tau^{\dagger}}{\left(1-2q\right)^{2}}\right\}.

(130)

Then, the Chow-Liu algorithm recovers the strong edges with probability at least
1-\delta
. In other words, it is true that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)\right]\geq 1-2p^{2}\exp\left(-\frac{n_{\dagger}\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}}{2}\right).

(131)

Proof.

From Lemma
19
, if there is an error then for an edge
f
not recovered in the tree
\mathrm{T}^{\text{CL}}_{\dagger}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}Z_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}M_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right)<0

Therefore one of the sums must be negative. Expanding, one of the two following inequalities must hold:

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}Z_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}-n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\geq n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right]

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\dagger}}Y_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}-n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\geq n_{\dagger}\mathbb{E}\left[M_{f,u,\bar{u}}^{(i)}\right].

In addition, (
115
), (
125
), Lemma
20
and Lemma
21
give the following pairs of inequalities:

\displaystyle\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\mu_{f}\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right\}

\displaystyle\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\mu_{f}\left(1+\mu_{A}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right\}

\displaystyle\left|\mu^{\dagger}_{f}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)^{-1}\max\left\{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right\}

\displaystyle\left|\mu^{\dagger}_{f}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\mu_{A}\right)^{-1}\max\left\{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2},4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}\right\}.

Putting these together:

\displaystyle\left|\mu^{\dagger}_{f}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\frac{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)},\frac{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}}{\left(1+\mu_{A}\right)},\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)},\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}}{\left(1+\mu_{A}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\frac{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)},\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)}.

(132)

We get the last inequality for non trivial values of the bound
\frac{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}}{\left(1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}\right)}\leq 1

and by using the following bound

\displaystyle\frac{8\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)}\leq\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}}{\sqrt{1-\mu_{A}}}=\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu_{A}}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)}\leq\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)}.

(133)

Finally the function
f(\mu_{A})=\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)}=\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\mu_{A}^{\text{}}}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)}

is increasing with respect to
\mu_{A}
(for all
\mu_{A}\leq 1
) and
\mu_{A}\leq\tanh\beta<1
, so we have

\displaystyle\left|\mu^{\dagger}_{f}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{A}}}{\left(1-\mu_{A}\right)}\leq\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\tanh\beta}}{\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)}\triangleq\tau^{\dagger}.

(134)

The weakest edge should satisfy
\left|\mu^{\dagger}_{f}\right|\geq\tau^{\dagger}
to guarantee
the correct recovery of the tree under the event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon_{\dagger}\right)
. This yields a condition on the edge strengths:

\displaystyle\left|\mu^{\dagger}_{f}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\tau^{\dagger}\implies

\displaystyle\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\tanh\alpha

\displaystyle\geq\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\tanh\beta}}{\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)}

\displaystyle\tanh\alpha

\displaystyle\geq\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\tanh\beta}}{\left(1-2q\right)^{2}\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)},\quad q\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).

(135)

The last inequality gives the definition of the strong edges in the noisy scheme.
∎

Based on the definition (
134
) we derive the following bound on
\tau^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\tau^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\tanh\beta}}{\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\epsilon_{\dagger}\sqrt{1-\left(1-8q+24q^{2}-32q^{3}+16q^{4}\right)\tanh\beta}}{\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 4\epsilon_{\dagger}\frac{\sqrt{1-\tanh\beta}+\sqrt{\left(1-3q+4q^{2}-2q^{3}\right)8q\tanh\beta}}{\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 4\epsilon_{\dagger}e^{\beta}\left(1+e^{\beta}\sqrt{\left(1-q\right)\left(2q^{2}-2q+1\right)8q\tanh\beta}\right)

(136)

\displaystyle<4\epsilon_{\dagger}e^{\beta}\left(1+2e^{\beta}\sqrt{2\left(1-q\right)q\tanh\beta}\right).

(137)

(
136
) holds because
1-\tanh(\beta)\geq e^{-2\beta}
. We will later use (
137
) in Lemma
25
.

In comparison to the noiseless setting (see
Bresler and Karzand 2020
), we can guarantee exact recover with high probability under the event
\mathrm{E}^{\text{strong}}\left(\epsilon\right)
when the weakest edge satisfies the inequality

\displaystyle\tanh\alpha\geq

\displaystyle\frac{4\epsilon}{\sqrt{1-\tanh\beta}}.

(138)

Notice that (
138
) can be obtained by (
135
) when
q=0
and
n=n_{\dagger}
. When
q>0
and
n=n_{\dagger}
it is clear that the set of trees that can be recovered from noisy
observations is a subset of the set of trees that can be recovered
from the original observations. Also, we have

\displaystyle\epsilon=\sqrt{2\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right)/n}

\displaystyle\implies n=\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\dagger}=\sqrt{2\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right)/n_{\dagger}}

\displaystyle\implies n_{\dagger}=\frac{2}{\epsilon_{\dagger}^{2}}\log\left(2p^{2}/\delta\right).

(139)

By combining (
135
) with (
139
) we found the number of samples that we need to recover the
tree with probality at
1-\delta
(Theorem
5
):

\displaystyle n_{\dagger}>

\displaystyle\frac{32\left[1-\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\tanh\beta\right]}{\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)^{2}\left(1-2q\right)^{4}\tanh^{2}\alpha}\log\frac{2p^{2}}{\delta}.

(140)

On the other hand when there is no noise
(
Bresler and Karzand 2020
)
we need

\displaystyle n>\frac{32}{\tanh^{2}\alpha\left(1-\tanh\beta\right)}\log\frac{2p^{2}}{\delta}.

(141)

Appendix D
Analysis of the Event
\mathrm{E}_{\dagger}^{\text{cascade}}\left(\gamma_{\dagger}\right)

Lemma 23
.

Consider a path of length
d\geq 2
in the original tree
\mathrm{T}
, and without loss of generality assume that path is
X_{1}-X_{2}-\cdot\cdot\cdot-X_{d+1}
. Recall that
Y^{(i)}_{m}
is the
i^{\text{th}}
sample of
Y_{m}
and
m\in[d+1]
and
\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}Y^{(i)}_{k}Y^{(i)}_{k+1}
,
k\in[d]
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Y^{(\ell)}_{k}Y^{(\ell)}_{k+1}=\pm 1\Big|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1},\ldots,\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{2}\frac{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-(\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1})^{2}}+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}\frac{1\pm\mu_{k}}{2}\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-(\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1})^{2}}.

(142)

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}Y^{(i)}_{k}Y^{(i)}_{k+1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(i)},

(143)

where eacch term

\displaystyle\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}\perp\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{r}\quad\forall r\in[1,2,\ldots k-2],\forall\ell\in[1,2,\dots,n]

(144)

thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\Big|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1},\ldots,\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\Big|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\Big|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(i)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(i)}\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(i)}\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right).

(145)

First we compute the probability
\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(i)}\right)
. Define the Bernoulli random variable
Z_{k-1}
as

\displaystyle Z_{k-1}\triangleq\frac{X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}+1}{2}=\begin{cases}0,\quad\text{w.p. }\frac{1-(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}}{2}\\
1,\quad\text{w.p. }\frac{1+(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}}{2}.\end{cases}

(146)

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(i)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\left(2Z_{k-1}-1\right)^{(i)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\sum^{n}_{i=1}Z_{k-1}^{(i)}=n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle={n\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}\left(\frac{1-(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n-n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}\left(\frac{1+(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}.

(147)

As a second step we compute the probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1}\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(i)}\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)

(148)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1,i\neq\ell}\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(i)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(\ell)}}{n}\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\Bigg).

Note that

\displaystyle\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(i)}\perp\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)},\quad\forall i\neq\ell,

(149)

and we would like to find the conditional distribution of
\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(\ell)}
under the event
\{\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(\ell)}=c\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell})=\pm 1\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(\ell)}=c,\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{1\pm c\mathbb{E}[X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}]+c(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{4}}{\frac{1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1\pm c(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\mu_{k}+c(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{2\left(1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}\right)},\quad c\in\{-1,+1\}.

(150)

Define

\displaystyle P_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(\ell)}=+1\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell})=\pm 1\right)

(151)

\displaystyle P_{2}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(\ell)}=-1\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell})=\pm 1\right),

(152)

then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1,i\neq\ell}\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(i)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\left(X_{k-1}N_{k-1}X_{k}N_{k}\right)^{(\ell)}}{n}\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum^{n}_{i=1,i\neq\ell}\left(2Z_{k-1}-1\right)^{(i)}+\frac{\left(2Z_{k-1}-1\right)^{(\ell)}}{n}\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\sum^{n}_{i=1,i\neq\ell}\left(Z_{k-1}\right)^{(i)}+\left(Z_{k-1}\right)^{(\ell)}=n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\left(Z_{k-1}\right)^{(\ell)}=0\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\sum^{n}_{i=1,i\neq\ell}\left(Z_{k-1}\right)^{(i)}=n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{P}\left(\left(Z_{k-1}\right)^{(\ell)}=1\Big|\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\sum^{n}_{i=1,i\neq\ell}\left(Z_{k-1}\right)^{(i)}=n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}-1\right)

\displaystyle=P_{2}{n-1\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}\left(\frac{1-(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n-1-n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}\left(\frac{1+(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+P_{1}{n-1\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1-(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n-1-n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}+1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\left(\frac{1+(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}-1}.

(153)

Finally

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)=\frac{1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{2},

(154)

and (
145
), (
147
), (
148
), (
153
) give

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(Y^{(\ell)}_{k}Y^{(\ell)}_{k+1}=\pm 1\Big|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1},\ldots,\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{1}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)}

\displaystyle=P_{2}{n-1\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}{n\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}^{-1}\frac{\left(\frac{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n-1-n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}\left(\frac{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}}{\left(\frac{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n-n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}\left(\frac{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\quad+P_{1}{n-1\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}-1}{n\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}^{-1}\frac{\left(\frac{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n-1-n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}+1}\left(\frac{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}-1}}{\left(\frac{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n-n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}\left(\frac{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}}

\displaystyle=P_{2}{n-1\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}{n\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}^{-1}\left(\frac{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad+P_{1}{n-1\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}-1}{n\choose n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}^{-1}\left(\frac{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=P_{2}\frac{n-n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}{n}\left(\frac{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{-1}+P_{1}\frac{n\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}+1}{2}}{n}\left(\frac{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{2}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=P_{2}\frac{1-\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}+P_{1}\frac{1+\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}.

(155)

The latter and the definition of
P_{1},P_{2}
(see Equations
151
and
152
) give

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Y^{(\ell)}_{k}Y^{(\ell)}_{k+1}\pm 1\Big|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1},\ldots,\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[P_{2}\frac{1-\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}+P_{1}\frac{1+\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}\right]\mathbb{P}\left(\left(X_{k}N_{k}X_{k+1}N_{k+1}\right)^{(\ell)}=\pm 1\right)

\displaystyle=\left[P_{2}\frac{1-\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}+P_{1}\frac{1+\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}\right]\frac{1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\frac{1\mp(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\mu_{k}-(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{2\left(1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}\right)}\frac{1-\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\mu_{k}+(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{2\left(1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}\right)}\frac{1+\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}\Bigg]\frac{1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1\mp(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\mu_{k}-(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{4}\frac{1-\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\mu_{k}+(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{4}\frac{1+\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{4}\left(\frac{1-\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}+\frac{1+\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\mu_{k}+(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}}{4}\left(\frac{1+\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1+\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}-\frac{1-\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1\pm(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k}}{2}\frac{1-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-(\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1})^{2}}+(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}\frac{1\pm\mu_{k}}{2}\frac{\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{k-1}-\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}}{1-(\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1})^{2}}.

(156)

Note that
(1-2q)^{2}\mu_{k-1}=\mu^{\dagger}_{k-1}
, and the proof is completed.

∎

Lemma 24
.

Define the function
K(\beta,q)
as

\displaystyle K(\beta,q)\triangleq\frac{10(1-\tanh^{2}(\beta))}{9+(1-2q)^{2}-\tanh^{2}(\beta)(1-2q)^{2}(9(1-2q)^{2}+1)}

(157)

and the event
\mathrm{E^{edge}_{e,\dagger}}
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{E^{edge}_{e,\dagger}}\triangleq\left\{\left|\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e}-\mu^{\dagger}_{e}\right|\leq\gamma_{e}\right\},\quad e\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}},\quad\gamma_{e}>0,

(158)

and
\mathrm{E^{edge}_{\dagger}(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}})}\triangleq\cap_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{T}}\mathrm{E^{edge}_{e,\dagger}}
. If

\displaystyle n\geq\frac{108e^{2\beta}\log(2p/\delta)}{(1-2q)^{4}K(\beta,q)}\text{ and }\gamma_{e}=\sqrt{3\frac{1-\mu_{e}^{2}}{nK(\beta,q)}\log(2p/\delta)}

(159)

then
\mathbb{P}\left[\left(\mathrm{E^{edge}_{\dagger}(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}})}\right)^{c}\right]\leq\delta
.

Proof.

The variance of
\hat{\mu}^{\dagger}_{e}
is
(1-(\mu^{\dagger}_{e})^{2})/n
and by applying Bernstein’s inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left(\mathrm{E^{edge}_{e,\dagger}}\right)^{c}\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{n\gamma^{2}_{e}}{2\left(1-(\mu^{\dagger}_{e})^{2}\right)+\frac{4}{3}\gamma_{e}}\right),\quad\forall\gamma_{e}>0.

(160)

We choose
\gamma_{e}=\sqrt{3\frac{1-\mu_{e}^{2}}{nK(\beta,q)}\log(2p/\delta)}
(because the parameter
\gamma_{e}
is free, that is, Bernstein’s inequality holds for all
\gamma_{e}>0
). If
n
satisfies (
159
) then

\displaystyle\gamma_{e}\leq\sqrt{3\frac{1-\mu_{e}^{2}}{108e^{2\beta}}(1-2q)^{4}}\leq\frac{(1-2q)^{2}}{6}(1-\mu_{e}^{2}),

(161)

and the last is true because
e^{-2\beta}\leq 1-\tanh(\beta)\leq 1-|\mu_{e}|\leq 1-\mu^{2}_{e}
. By applying (
159
) and (
161
) on (
160
) we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left(\mathrm{E^{edge}_{e,\dagger}}\right)^{c}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{n\gamma^{2}_{e}}{2\left(1-(\mu^{\dagger}_{e})^{2}\right)+\frac{4}{3}\gamma_{e}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{3\frac{1-\mu_{e}^{2}}{K(\beta,q)}\log(2p/\delta)}{2\left(1-(1-2q)^{4}\mu_{e}^{2}\right)+\frac{4}{3}\frac{(1-2q)^{2}}{6}(1-\mu^{2}_{e})}\right)

\displaystyle=2\exp\left(-\frac{3}{K(\beta,q)}\frac{1-\mu_{e}^{2}}{2+\frac{2}{9}(1-2q)^{2}-\mu_{e}^{2}(1-2q)^{2}(2(1-2q)^{2}+\frac{2}{9})}\log(2p/\delta)\right)

\displaystyle=2\exp\left(-\frac{3}{2K(\beta,q)}\frac{1-\mu_{e}^{2}}{1+\frac{1}{9}(1-2q)^{2}-\mu_{e}^{2}(1-2q)^{2}((1-2q)^{2}+\frac{1}{9})}\log(2p/\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{10}{9K(\beta,q)}\frac{1-\mu_{e}^{2}}{1+\frac{1}{9}(1-2q)^{2}-\mu_{e}^{2}(1-2q)^{2}((1-2q)^{2}+\frac{1}{9})}\log(2p/\delta)\right).

(162)

The following function

\displaystyle f(x)=\frac{10}{9}\frac{1-x}{1+\frac{1}{9}(1-2q)^{2}-x(1-2q)^{2}((1-2q)^{2}+\frac{1}{9})},\text{ }x\in[\tanh^{2}(\alpha),\tanh^{2}(\beta)]

(163)

is strictly decreasing, thus we have
f(\tanh^{2}(\beta))\leq f(x)
for all
x\in[\tanh^{2}(\alpha),\tanh^{2}(\beta)]
. Also
K(\beta,q)\equiv f(\tanh^{2}(\beta))
, the latter together with (
162
) give

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left(\mathrm{E^{edge}_{e,\dagger}}\right)^{c}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{K(\beta,q)}f(\mu^{2}_{e})\log(2p/\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{K(\beta,q)}f(\tanh^{2}(\beta))\log(2p/\delta)\right)

\displaystyle=2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{K(\beta,q)}K(\beta,q)\log(2p/\delta)\right)
